Constructing the quantum queer supergroup using Hecke-Clifford superalgebras
Jie Du, Haixia Gu, Zhenhua Li and Jinkui Wan

Abstract
. In [ DGLW ] , we use certain special elements and their commutation relations in the Hecke-Clifford algebras ℋ r , R c \mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R} to derive some fundamental multiplication formulas associated with the natural bases in queer q q -Schur superalgebras 𝒬 q ​ ( n , r , R ) \mathcal{Q}_{q}(n,r;R) introduced in [ DW2 ] . Here a natural basis element is defined by a special element T A ⋆ T_{A^{\!\star}} in ℋ r , R c \mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R} associated with a pair of certain n × n n\times n matrices A ⋆ = ( A 0 ¯ | A 1 ¯ ) {A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}}) over ℕ \mathbb{N} with entries sum to r r . The definition of T A ⋆ T_{A^{\!\star}} consists of an element c A ⋆ c_{A^{\!\star}} in the Clifford superalgebra and an element T A T_{A} in the Hecke algebra, where A = A 0 ¯ + A 1 ¯ A=A^{\bar{0}}+A^{\bar{1}} . Note that all T A T_{A} can be used to define the natural basis for the corresponding q q -Schur algebra 𝒮 q ​ ( n , r ) {\mathcal{S}}_{q}(n,r) . This paper is a continuation of [ DGLW ] . We start with standardized queer υ {\upsilon} -Schur superalgebras 𝒬 υ s ​ ( n , r ) \mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r) , for R = ℤ ⁡ [ υ , υ − 1 ] R=\mathbb{Z}[{\upsilon},{\upsilon}^{-1}] and q = υ 2 q={\upsilon}^{2} , and their natural bases. With the υ {\upsilon} -Schur algebra 𝒮 υ ​ ( n , r ) {\mathcal{S}}_{\upsilon}(n,r) at the background, the first key ingredient is a standardisation of the natural basis for 𝒬 υ s ​ ( n , r ) \mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r) and their associated standard multiplication formulas. By introducing some long elements of finite sums, we then extend the formulas to these long elements which allow us to explicitly define ℚ ⁡ ( υ ) \mathbb{Q}({\upsilon}) -superalgebra homomorphisms 𝝃 n , r \bm{\xi}_{n,r} from the quantum queer supergroup 𝑼 v ​ ( 𝔮 n ) {\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n}) to queer q q -Schur superalgebras 𝓠 υ s ​ ( n , r ) \bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r) , for all r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 . Finally, taking limits of long elements yields certain infinitely long elements as formal infinite series which eventually lead to a new construction for 𝑼 v ​ ( 𝔮 n ) {\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n}) .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
17B37, 17A70, 20G42, 20C08

1.
Introduction

1.1
.

Finite algebra approach to quantum groups.

Since the introduction of quantum groups as quantized enveloping algebras
of semisimple Lie algebras in late eighties of the last century, their realizations and related
applications have achieved outstanding progress. First, C. M. Ringel developed a realization
for the positive part of such a quantum group via Ringel–Hall algebras
associated with the representation category of finite quiver (or path) algebras.
Then G. Lusztig’s geometric approach to quantum groups and canonical bases advanced the theory to a new level.

On the other hand, almost at the same time,
Beilinson, Lusztig and MacPherson
[
BLM
]
initiated a new method
of using a sequence of geometrically defined convolution algebras associated
with some (finite) partial flag varieties (i.e., finite
q
-Schur algebras) to approach quantum linear groups
\mathbf{U}=\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.
In their work, certain structure constants relative to the orbital basis satisfy a stabilisation property. This property results in the definition of an infinite dimensional idempotented algebra—the modified quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
.
Then, by a process of taking limit, it
results in a new realization for the entire quantum group
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

via its regular representation defined explicitly by
some multiplication formulas of a basis by generators.

The convolution algebra approach was soon generalized to quantum affine
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
in
[
GV
,
L
]
,
which motivated, with a modification of the original BLM approach in
[
DF1
]
, an algebraic approach to a new realization of the quantum loop algebra of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
; see
[
DDF
,
DF2
]
.
Recently, Bao and Wang
[
BW
]
investigated
i
-quantum groups
\bm{U}^{\jmath}
and
\bm{U}^{\imath}
(or more precisely, certain quantum symmetric pairs of type AIII) to give a reformulation of Kazhdan-Lusztig theory that provides a
perfect solution to the problem of character formulas of
\mathfrak{osp}
Lie superalgebras. Motivated from the Bao-Wang’s work,
the finite convolution algebra approach has also been generalized to
the type B/C geometry and to the modified
i
-quantum groups and their canonical bases
in
[
BKLW
]
. Thus, the new Schur–Weyl duality in
[
BW
]
further motivates
BLM type realizations for the above mentioned twin
i
-quantum groups; see
[
DWu1
,
DWu2
]
.

1.2
.

A super construction
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
.

Though the geometric (or convolution algebra) approach seems not available for quantum supergroups,
the idea of using finite dimensional superalgebras to approach quantum supergroups
continues to shed lights on the development of the algebraic approach. For example, the work
[
DGZ
]
shows that
the desired fundamental multiplication formulas can be rooted out from the associated Hecke algebras (see
[
DG
,
DGZ
]
) via the quantum Schur–Weyl duality. In other words, these multiplication formulas can be derived by certain special elements and commutation relations in the associated Hecke algebras. Thus, the new construction for
\mathbf{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
or the quantum supergroup
\mathbf{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
can be built on some structures in the Hecke algebras. This tells that the structure of these quantum groups is actually hidden in the structure of Hecke algebras.

More precisely,
the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{r,R}
of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
over a commutative ring
R
has the following fundamental structure: it is generated by
T_{s_{1}},\ldots,T_{s_{r-1}}
and has basis
\{T_{w}\mid w\in\mathfrak{S}_{r}\}
with respect to which we have the following matrix representation of the regular module
{}_{{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathcal{H}}
:

T_{s_{i}}T_{w}=\begin{cases}T_{s_{i}w},&\text{ if }s_{i}w>w;\\
(q-1)T_{w}+qT_{s_{i}w},&\text{ if }s_{i}w<w.\end{cases}

Building on the aforementioned special elements and commutation relations, one may construct a new basis
\{A({\bm{j}})\}_{A,{\bm{j}}}
for the quantum linear supergroups
\bm{U}=\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
, containing its generators
K_{j},E_{h},F_{h}
,
such that
the structure constants relative to the basis appearing in

K_{j}A({\bm{j}}),\;\;E_{h}A({\bm{j}}),\;\;F_{h}A({\bm{j}})

can be explicitly computed; see
[
BLM
, Lem. 5.3]
for the quantum linear group (
n=0
) case and
[
DGZ
, Cor. 4.4]
and
[
DG
, Prop. 5.5-6, Th. 8.4]
in general. In other words, this construction, symbolised as
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
with
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}={\mathcal{H}}_{r,\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})}
, shows that the structure of
\bm{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
is hidden in that of
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}
. It is natural to ask if there exists a similar hidden structural connection
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
for the quantum queer supergroup
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
and Hecke-Clifford superalgebras
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}:={\mathcal{H}}^{c}_{r,\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})}
.

1.3
.

A roadmap for
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
.

In this paper, we give an affirmative answer to the question. By displaying a construction
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
that presents the quantum queer supergroup
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
via Hecke-Clifford algebras
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
, we show
that the structure of the quantum queer supergroup
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
is hidden in the Hecke–Clifford superalgebra
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
.

We now describe a roadmap for the construction
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
in the following steps
1
1

1

Note that the geometric approach initiated in
[
BLM
]
is a shortcut to the construction
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n})
, where steps (1)–(3) are combined in terms of convolution algebras and convolution product.
:

(1)

Constructing some special elements and
commutation formulas in the Hecke–Clifford superalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
.

(2)

Defining queer
q
-Schur superalgebras
\mathcal{Q}_{q}(n,r)
and its natural basis
\{\phi_{A^{\!\star}}\}_{A^{\!\star}}
.

(3)

Using (1) to compute some fundamental multiplication formulas (MFs) in
\mathcal{Q}_{q}(n,r)
.

(4)

Standardizing everything:
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
, standard basis
[{A^{\!\star}}]
, and new MFs.

(5)

Introducing long elements
\{{A^{\!\star}}(\bm{j},r)\}_{{A^{\!\star}},\bm{j}}
and deriving their multiplication formulas in
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
to establish homomorphisms
\bm{\xi}_{n,r}:\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})\rightarrow\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
for
r\geq 1
.

(6)

Establishing triangular relations to determine the image of
\bm{\xi}_{n}:\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})\rightarrow\prod_{r\geq 1}\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
.

(7)

Completing the construction by proving the injectivity of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
.

Note that steps (1)–(3) have been completed in
[
DW2
]
and
[
DGLW
]
. Building on
[
DGLW
]
, we complete the remaining steps in this paper.

The following theorem is the main result of the paper which gives a new basis for the quantum queer supergroup and their structure constants for generators.
Let
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
denote the standard basis for
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and let

{\alpha}_{i}=\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}\text{ and }{\alpha}^{+}_{i}=\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{i+1}.

Then
{\alpha}_{i}
, for
i\in[1,n-1]
are known as the “simple roots”.

1.4
.

Main Theorem.

The quantum queer supergroup
{\bm{U}}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
, generated by
\mathsf{E}_{h},\mathsf{F}_{h},\mathsf{K}_{i},\mathsf{K}_{i}^{-1},\mathsf{K}_{\overline{n}}
, for
h,i\in[1,n],h\neq n
, has a basis

\{{A^{\!\star}}({\bm{j}})\mid{A^{\!\star}}=(a_{i,j}^{\overline{0}}|a_{i,j}^{\overline{1}})\in M_{n}({\mathbb{N}}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})^{\pm},{\bm{j}}=(j_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}\vskip-4.30554pt

that satisfies the following multiplication rules:

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{i}^{\pm 1}\cdot{A^{\!\star}}(\bm{j})={v}^{\pm\sum_{u=1}^{n}a_{h,u}}{A^{\!\star}}(\bm{j}\pm\epsilon_{i}),

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{h}\cdot{A^{\!\star}}(\bm{j})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k<h}{{\upsilon}}^{f_{h,k}^{\overline{0}}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1](A^{\overline{0},+}_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})(\bm{j}+{\alpha}_{h})+\sum_{h+1<k}{{\upsilon}}^{f_{h,k}^{\overline{0}}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1](A^{\overline{0},+}_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})(\bm{j})

\displaystyle+\frac{{{\upsilon}}^{f_{h,h}^{\overline{0}}-j_{h}}}{{\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1}}\left\{({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h+1,h}|{A}^{\bar{1}})(\bm{j}+{\alpha}_{h})-({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h+1,h}|{A}^{\bar{1}})(\bm{j}-{\alpha}_{h}^{+})\right\}

\displaystyle+{{\upsilon}}^{f_{h,h+1}^{\overline{0}}+j_{h+1}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,h+1}+1]({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,h+1}|{A}^{\bar{1}})(\bm{j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k<h}

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle\upsilon}}^{f_{h,k}^{\overline{1}}}({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h+1,k}+E_{h,k})(\bm{j}+{\alpha}_{h})+{{\upsilon}}^{f_{h,h}^{\overline{1}}}({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h+1,h}+E_{h,h})(\bm{j}-\epsilon_{h+1})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{k>h+1}{{\upsilon}}^{f^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}}({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1})\left[{a_{h,k}}+1\atop 2\right]({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})(\bm{j}).

\displaystyle(3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{h}\cdot{A^{\!\star}}(\bm{j})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k<h}{\upsilon}^{g_{h,k}^{\bar{0}}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}+1]({{A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}+E_{h+1,k}}|{{A}^{\bar{1}}})(\bm{j})+{{\upsilon}}^{g_{h,h}^{\bar{0}}+j_{h}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,h}+1]({{A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h+1,h}}|{{A}^{\bar{1}}})(\bm{j})

.

1.5
.

Remarks on the history of the project.

This project was initiated almost ten years ago. The main obstacles occurred in deriving fundamental multiplication formulas for odd generators. After some unsuccessful early attempts in 2014, we realised that the computation for the multiplication formulas in the odd case is almost impossible. Thus, we suspected the existence of such a construction! A couple of years later, we tried to tackle the problem in two different pre-projects. First, establish the construction in the classical (i.e.,
{v}=1
) case and second, develop a direct construction of the regular representation of
\bm{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
, using
{\upsilon}
-differential operators. Both projects were successful.

In
[
GLL
]
, a new realisation of the enveloping superalgebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
using Sergeev superalgebras is obtained by following closely the aforementioned roadmap. On the other hand, motivated from the examples given in
[
J
, §5A.7-6]
, a direct construction of the regular representation, using
{\upsilon}
-differential operators, was first tested for quantum
\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n}
in
[
DZ
]
and then generalised to the queer case in
[
DLZ
]
. The two works convinced us such a construction
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\rightsquigarrow\bm{U}_{\upsilon}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
, using Hecke–Clifford algebras
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
, must exist. Thus, we completed a preliminary version (see unpublished manuscript
[
dglw
]
) in 2022. In this work, standardization was not considered and a complicated order relation was used in the triangular relations! With another two-year efforts, we finally discovered how to normalize the natural basis to obtain the standard basis and to replace the ugly order with the order defined as an easy extension of the well-known order in
[
BLM
]
.

It should be pointed out that, unlike the classical case in
[
GLL
]
, we still could not write down completely all multiplication formulas
involving odd generators because of the complexity caused by the Clifford part. This
shows a drastic difference from the case of
\mathfrak{gl}_{m|n}
.
However, by introducing the SDP condition
(see
[
DGLW
, Def. 4.2]
), we derive some fundamental multiplication formulas
under the SDP condition; see
[
DGLW
, Th. 6.2–4]
.
2
2

2

We will standardise these formula in this paper.

Fortunately, by an inductive approach (cf. Remark
3.2
),
this is enough to guarantee that the generators and relations for the quantum queer supergroup
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
all hold in queer
{\upsilon}
-Schur superalgebras.
In this way, together with an application of the PBW type basis established in
[
DW1
]
, we successfully completed Steps (4)–(8) in the roadmap.
Note that the combinatorics developed in
[
DGZ
]
and
[
DW1
,
DW2
]
played decisive roles.

1.6
.

Further applications.

In forthcoming papers, we will apply
this new construction via queer
q
-Schur algebras to establish the integral Schur–Weyl–Olshanski duality and
to develop the modular representation theory of quantum queer supergroups at roots of unity, generalizing the work
[
BK1
,
BK2
]
to the quantum case. We will also use a bar involution, whose definition relies heavily on our theory, to establish the canonical basis theory for polynomial representations (cf.
[
GJKK
,
GJKKK
]
) and for the modified supergroup
\dot{\bm{U}}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
.

1.7
.

Organization.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
,
we recall some basics on the natural bases for queer
q
-Schur superalgebras
\mathcal{Q}_{q}(n,r;R)
and their standardized (or signed) version

{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
. We further show the two superalgebras are isomorphism if
\sqrt{-1}\in R
.
Section
3
starts with a Drinfeld–Jimbo type presentation for the quantum queer supergroup
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
and Olshanski’s epimorphism from
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
to
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r):=\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{q}(n,r;R)
for
R=\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon}),q={\upsilon}^{2}
.
The integral version
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r):=\mathcal{Q}_{{\upsilon}^{2}}^{s}(n,r;{\mathcal{Z}})
(
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}[{\upsilon},{\upsilon}^{-1}]
) of
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
admits a standard basis. This basis is a normalization of the natural basis for
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
, a key ingredient which has no geometric interpretation.
In Section
4
,
we derive the multiplication formulas of the standard basis by certain homogeneous generators for the superalgebra

\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
.
Similar to BLM’s original method, we introduce
in Section
5
some “long elements” which span the algebra
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
over
\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})
uniformly on
r
and derive the corresponding multiplication formulas.
Note that the “structure constants” in these multiplication formulas are independent of
r
. We then check all the defining relations of
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
in
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
in Section
6
.
In this way, we obtain superalgebra homomorphisms
\bm{\xi}_{n,r}
from
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
to
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
(Theorem
6.3
).

The aim of the remaining three sections is to prove the main Theorem
1.4
. The homomorphisms
\bm{\xi}_{n,r}
induces a homomorphism
\bm{\xi}_{n}
from
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
to the direct product
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{{\upsilon}}({n})
of
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{{\upsilon}}({n},{r})
. The determination of the image of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
requires a triangular transition matrix between a monomial basis and the standard basis for
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
. This is done in Theorem
7.13
.
A key ingredient for the triangular relations is the selection of an order relation. For example, in a preliminary version of the work
[
dglw
]
, a very complicated order was used. The new order relation
\prec^{\star}
in Definition
7.3
is an easy extension of the order relation used for quantum
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

[
BLM
]
. In Section 8, by taking limits of long elements, we obtain infinitely long elements (as formal infinite series) which span a subspace
{\bm{\mathfrak{A}}_{v}(n)}
of
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{{\upsilon}}({n})
. We then use the extended triangular relations to prove that
\text{im}(\bm{\xi}_{n})={\bm{\mathfrak{A}}_{v}(n)}
(Theorem
8.3
).
Finally, in Section
9
,
we use the triangular decomposition of
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
together with a certain weight space decomposition to prove that
\bm{\xi}_{n}
is an isomorphism.
Thus, the new superalgebra
{\bm{\mathfrak{A}}_{v}(n)}
becomes a new realization of
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
, proving the main Theorem
1.4
.

1.8
.

Some standard notations.

Denote by
\mathbb{N}
(resp.,
\mathbb{Z}
) the set of non-negative integers (resp., integers) and let
\mathbb{N}_{2}=\{0,1\}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\bar{0},\bar{1}\}
. Let
{{\upsilon}}
be an indeterminate,
{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[{\upsilon},{\upsilon}^{-1}]
, and
\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})
the fraction field of
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

For any positive integers
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

(1.8.1)

\displaystyle[m]=\frac{{\upsilon}^{m}-{\upsilon}^{-m}}{{\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1}},\quad[m]!=[m][m-1]\cdots[1],\quad\left[m\atop s\right]=\frac{[m]!}{[s]![m-s]!}

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle[\![{m}]\!]}}=1+{q}+\cdots+{q}^{m-1}=\frac{{q}^{m}-1}{{q}-1}\;\;(q={\upsilon}^{2}).

For
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
i\leq j
, let
[i,j]=\{i,i+1,\cdots,j\}
and
[i,j)=[i,j-1]
, for
i<j
.

2.
Preliminaries

We start with the definition of a Hecke-Clifford superalgebra.
Let
R
be a commutative ring such that
2\in R^{\times}
(the group of units in
R
).
Assume
r\in\mathbb{N}
is a positive integer, and let

\mathfrak{S}_{r}
be the symmetric group on
r
letters with Coxeter generator
s_{i}=(i,i+1)
, for all
i\in[1,r)
.

Let
q\in R^{\times}
.
The
Hecke-Clifford superalgebra

\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
is a superalgebra over
{R}
generated by
even generators
T_{i}=T_{s_{i}}
for all
1\leq i<r
, and odd generators
c_{1},\cdots,c_{r}
,
subject to the relations

(2.0.1)

\displaystyle c_{i}^{2}=-1,\quad c_{i}c_{j}=-c_{j}c_{i},\qquad(1\leq i,j\leq r-1);

\displaystyle(T_{i}-{q})(T_{i}+1)=0,\qquad T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i},\qquad(q\in R,\;|i-j|>1),

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1},\qquad(i\neq r-1);

\displaystyle T_{i}c_{j}=c_{j}T_{i},\qquad(j\neq i,i+1);

\displaystyle T_{i}c_{i}=c_{i+1}T_{i},\qquad T_{i}c_{i+1}=c_{i}T_{i}-({q}-1)(c_{i}-c_{i+1}).

The subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{r,R}
generated by even generators is the Hecke algebra associated with
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
and has
basis
\{T_{w}\mid w\in\mathfrak{S}_{r}\}
, where
T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{l}}
if
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
is reduced.

For any homogeneous element
h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
, let
\wp({h})\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{2}
be its parity, more precisely,
\wp({h})=\bar{0}
if
h
is even, or
\wp({h})=\overline{1}
if
h
is odd. From Section
3
onwards, we will only consider the generic version
{\mathcal{H}}_{r}^{c}={\mathcal{H}}_{r,{\mathcal{Z}}}^{c}
and

\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}={\mathcal{H}}_{r,\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})}^{c}
, where
q={\upsilon}^{2}
.

We now introduce some special elements in
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
building on which the structure constants of the generators of
\bm{U}_{\!{\upsilon}}(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
relative to a new basis can be computed near the end of the paper.

First, the Young (or parabolic) subgroups of
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
are defined by compositions of
r
.
Recall the set of compositions of
r
into
n
parts:

(2.0.2)

\Lambda(n,r)=\Big\{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\ \bm{|}\ |\lambda|:=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=r\Big\}.

Associated with
{\lambda}=({\lambda}_{1},\cdots,{\lambda}_{n})\in\Lambda(n,r)
, define its
partial sum sequence

(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\lambda}_{n})
, where

(2.0.3)

\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\lambda_{k}\;\;(1\leq i\leq n),\text{ and set }\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}=0.

Then the Young subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
is the subgroup

\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}:=\langle s_{i}\mid i\in[1,r)\setminus\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}\mid j\in[1,n)\}\rangle.

Let
x_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}T_{w}
. These special elements are used to define the right
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
-module
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
. An

\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
-module homomorphisms
\phi:M\to M
is defined to satisfy
\phi(mh)=\phi(m)h
, for all
m\in M
and
h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
and all such homomorphisms define,
following
[
DW2
]
, the
queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
over
R

(2.0.4)

\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R):=\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\Big).

Second,
we introduce some special
c
-elements
c_{q,i,j},c_{\nu}^{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
to describe bases for
x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\cap\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}x_{\mu}
and, hence, for
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
.
By definition, for
1\leq i<j\leq r
, we set

c_{q,i,j}=q^{j-i}c_{i}+q^{j-i-1}c_{i+1}+\cdots+qc_{j-1}+c_{j},\quad c^{\prime}_{q,i,j}=c_{i}+qc_{i+1}+\cdots+q^{j-i}c_{j}

Putting
c_{q,r}=c_{q,1,r}
and
c_{q,r}^{\prime}=c_{q,1,r}^{\prime}
, we have the first commutation relations:
x_{(r)}c_{q,r}=c^{\prime}_{q,r}x_{(r)}
. Thus, the set
\{x_{(r)},x_{(r)}c_{q,r}\}
forms a basis for
x_{(r)}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\cap\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}x_{(r)}
.

For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
and
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2}^{m}
with
m\geq 1
, the following elements introduced in
[
DW2
, §4]

(2.0.5)

\displaystyle c^{\alpha}_{\lambda}={(c_{{q},1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}})}^{\alpha_{1}}{(c_{{q},\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}+1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}})}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots{(c_{{q},\widetilde{\lambda}_{m-1}+1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{m}})}^{\alpha_{m}},

\displaystyle(c^{\alpha}_{\lambda})^{\prime}={(c^{\prime}_{{q},1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}})}^{\alpha_{1}}{(c^{\prime}_{{q},\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}+1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{2}})}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots{(c^{\prime}_{{q},\widetilde{\lambda}_{m-1}+1,\widetilde{\lambda}_{m}})}^{\alpha_{m}}.

Here we use the convention
x^{0}=1
.
By
[
DW2
, Lem. 4.1, Cor. 4.3]
,
we have commutation relations

(2.0.6)

\displaystyle x_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}^{\alpha}=(c_{\lambda}^{\alpha})^{\prime}x_{\lambda},

for all
\alpha\leq\lambda
(meaning
\alpha_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for any
i
),
and the set
\{x_{{\lambda}}c_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}_{2},\alpha\leq{\lambda}\}
forms a basis for

x_{{\lambda}}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\lambda},R}^{c}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{{\lambda},R}^{c}x_{{\lambda}}
, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\lambda},R}^{c}
is the parabolic subalgebra associated with
{\lambda}
.

Finally, we describe a basis for
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
.

Denote by
M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
the set of
n\times n
-matrices with entries being non-negative integers. For any
A=(a_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
, set
|A|=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}
and

(2.0.7)

\mathrm{ro}(A)=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{1,j},\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{2,j},\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{n,j}\Big),\quad\mathrm{co}(A)=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,1},\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,2},\ldots,\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,n}\Big).

Let
{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2}=\{0,1\}\subset\mathbb{N}
. Recall the following matrix sets introduced in
[
DW2
]
:

(2.0.8)

\displaystyle M_{n}(\mathbb{N})_{r}=\{A=(a_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})\mid r=|A|\},

\displaystyle M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})=\{{A^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}})\ \bm{|}\ {A}^{\bar{0}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}),{A}^{\bar{1}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}_{2})\},

\displaystyle M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}=\{{A^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})\ \bm{|}\ {A}^{\bar{0}}+{A}^{\bar{1}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})_{r}\}.

Define the parity map
\wp

:M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as follows: for any
{A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})
,

(2.0.9)

\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})=|{A}^{\bar{1}}|\;\mbox{mod }2=\sum_{i,j}a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}\;\mbox{mod }2.

For
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
, let
A=A^{\bar{0}}+A^{\bar{1}}
, which is called the
base
of
{A^{\!\star}}
. Suppose

A^{\bar{0}}=({a}^{\bar{0}}_{i,j})
,
A^{\bar{1}}=({a}^{\bar{1}}_{i,j})
, and
A=(a_{i,j})
with
a_{i,j}=a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}+a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}
.
By concatenating columns, we set

(2.0.10)

\displaystyle\nu_{A}:=(a_{1,1},\ldots,a_{n,1},\ldots,a_{1,n},\ldots,a_{n,n}),

and its associated partial sum sequence (see (
2.0.3
))

(2.0.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{A}=(\widetilde{a}_{1,1},\ldots,\widetilde{a}_{i,j},\ldots,\widetilde{a}_{n,n}),\;\text{ where }\;\widetilde{a}_{i,j}:=\sum_{p=1}^{j-1}\sum_{u=1}^{n}a_{u,p}+\sum_{u=1}^{i}a_{u,j}.

For
\nu=\nu_{A}
and
\nu^{\bar{1}}=\nu_{A^{\bar{1}}}
, define

(2.0.12)

\displaystyle c_{{A^{\!\star}}}

\displaystyle=c_{\nu}^{\nu^{\bar{1}}},\qquad c^{\prime}_{{A^{\!\star}}}=(c_{\nu}^{\nu^{\bar{1}}})^{\prime},\qquad

\displaystyle T_{{A^{\!\star}}}

\displaystyle=x_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}T_{d_{A}}c_{{A^{\!\star}}}\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{{\nu}_{A}}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{co}(A)}}T_{\sigma},

where
d_{A}
is the distinguished (or shortest) representative of the double coset
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}d_{A}\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
(
{\lambda}={\mathrm{ro}(A)},\mu={\mathrm{co}(A)}
) defined by matrix
A
; (see
[
DDPW
, §4.2]
). Note that
\wp({T_{A^{\!\star}}})=\wp({c_{A^{\!\star}}})=\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})
.

By
[
DW2
, Prop. 5.2]
, for any
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
,
the intersection
x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\cap\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}x_{\mu}
is a free
R
-module with basis
\{T_{A^{\!\star}}\mid{A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r},\lambda=\mathrm{ro}(A),\mu=\mathrm{co}(A)\}
. Thus, we have the following.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
DW2
, Th. 5.3]
)
.

The queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
is a free
R
-module with
natural basis

\{\phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}\ \bm{|}\ {A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}\}
, where
\phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}
is defined by

\phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}:\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R},\;\;x_{\mu}h\longmapsto\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}T_{{A^{\!\star}}}h,\;\forall\mu\in\Lambda(n,r),h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R},

and
\phi_{A^{\!\star}}
has parity
\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})
.

We observe that, for
{A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
,

(2.1.1)

\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}\neq 0\implies\mathrm{co}(B)=\mathrm{ro}(A).

Note that this natural basis for
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
is an natural extension of the double coset basis of
q
-Schur algebras or superalgebras.

Fundamental multiplication formulas are referred to writing the product
\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}
as a linear combination of natural basis elements for certain choices of
{B^{\!\star}}
:

(2.1.2)

\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\cdot\phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}\gamma_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}^{{M^{\!\star}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}.

According to the generators for the queer Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{q}_{n}:=\mathfrak{q}(n,\mathbb{Q})
,
{B^{\!\star}}
is one of the following six matrices

(\mu+E_{j,j}|O),(\mu+E_{h,h+1}|O),(\mu+E_{h+1,h}|O);\quad(\mu|E_{j,j}),(\mu|E_{h,h+1}),(\mu|E_{h+1,h}),

where
\mu\in\Lambda(n,r-1)
stands for the diagonal matrix diag
(\mu)
and
E_{i,j}
denotes the
n\times n
matrix unit with entry
1
at
(i,j)
position and
0
elsewhere. Here
we also use
O
to denote the zero matrix in
M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
or
M_{n}({{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})
, and set
{O^{\star}}=({O}|{O})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})
.

In the even case, where
{B^{\!\star}}\in\{(\mu+E_{j,j}|O),(\mu+E_{h,h+1}|O),(\mu+E_{h+1,h}|O)\}
, (
2.1.2
) is given in
[
DGLW
, Th. 5.3]
; while in the odd case, where
{B^{\!\star}}\in\{(\mu|E_{j,j}),(\mu|E_{h,h+1}),(\mu|E_{h+1,h})\}
, (
2.1.2
) is given in
[
DGLW
, Th. 6.2–4]
.

The multiplication formulas in the odd case only display “the head terms” defined by a so-called SDP condition. The SDP condition is about commuting a Clifford generator with an element in
{\mathcal{H}}_{r,R}
associated with the distinguished double coset representative.

Recall the following bijection (see e.g.,
[
DDPW
, Section 4.2]
)

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}:M_{n}(\mathbb{N})_{r}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{(\lambda,d,\mu)\ \bm{|}\ \lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(n,r),d\in\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,\mu}\},\quad A\longmapsto(\mathrm{ro}(A),d_{A},\mathrm{co}(A)).

where
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,\mu}
is the set of distinguish representatives of the
(\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{S}_{\mu})
double coset in
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
. The representative
d_{A}
of minimal length in the double coset can be defined as follows.

For
A=(a_{ij})
, recall from (
2.0.11
) the column entry sum
\widetilde{a}_{i,j}
up to (and including)
a_{i,j}
. We now define

\widetilde{a}^{r}_{ij}
to be the entry sum along row 1 and row 2, etc., up to (and including)
a_{i,j}
. Then
3
3

3

The row entry partial sum
\widetilde{a}^{r}_{ij}
is denoted as
\widehat{a}_{i,j}
in
[
DGLW
]
.

d_{A}({\widetilde{a}}_{h-1,k}+p)=\widetilde{a}^{r}_{h,k-1}+p
,
for all
h,k\in[1,n]
with
a_{h,k}>0
and
p\in[1,a_{h,k}]
. In other words, as a permutation, we have

d_{A}=\displaystyle\prod_{h,k\atop a_{h,k}>0}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\widetilde{a}_{h-1,k}+1&\widetilde{a}_{h-1,k}+2&\cdots&\widetilde{a}_{h-1,k}+a_{h,k}\\
\widetilde{a}^{r}_{h,k-1}+1&\widetilde{a}^{r}_{h,k-1}+2&\cdots&\widetilde{a}^{r}_{h,k-1}+a_{h,k}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We say that
A

satisfies the
semi-direct product
(SDP)
condition
at
(h,k)
with
a_{h,k}>0
(or at a non-zero
(h,k)
-entry) if, for each
p\in[1,a_{h,k}]
,

c_{\widetilde{a}^{r}_{h,k-1}+p}{T_{d_{A}}}={T_{d_{A}}}c_{\widetilde{a}_{h-1,k}+p}\qquad(\mbox{in }\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}).

If
A
satisfies the SDP condition at
(h,k)
, for all
k\in[1,n]
with
a_{h,k}>0
, then we say that
A
satisfies the SDP condition on the
h
-th row.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
DGLW
, Th. 4.6, Cor. 4.7]
)
.

Let
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
and
h,k\in[1,n]
.
Then
A
satisfies the SDP condition at
(h,k)
if and only if
a_{h,k}>0
and
a_{i,j}=0
, for
i>h
and
j<k
(equivalently, the lower left corner matrix
A_{\text{\normalsize$\urcorner$}}^{h,k}
at
(h,k)
is 0). In particular,
A
satisfies the SDP condition on the
n
-th row.

For
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\overline{0}}|A^{\overline{1}})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
,
i\in{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2}
, and
1\leq h\leq n-1,1\leq k\leq n
, let

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle A^{\overline{i},+}_{h,k}=A^{\overline{i}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k},\qquad A^{\overline{i},-}_{h,k}=A^{\overline{i}}-E_{h,k}+E_{h+1,k}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}).

If
A=A^{\overline{0}}+A^{\overline{1}}
, then
(A^{\overline{0},+}_{h,k}|A^{\overline{1}})
and
(A^{\overline{0}}|A^{\overline{1},+}_{h,k})
, resp.,
(A^{\overline{0},-}_{h,k}|A^{\overline{1}})
and
(A^{\overline{0}}|A^{\overline{1},-}_{h,k})
has base

(2.2.2)

A^{+}_{h,k}=A+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}\;\;\text{ resp. }A^{-}_{h,k}=A-E_{h,k}+E_{h+1,k}.

Each of these matrices is obtained by moving 1 up or down a row in column
k
. We call them a
1-up matrix
or
1-down matrix
.

Remark 2.3
.

If we only consider the fundamental multiplication formulas (
2.1.2
) as given in
[
DGLW
, Ths. 5.3, 6.3–4]
for
{B^{\!\star}}\in\{(\mu+E_{h,h+1}|O),(\mu+E_{h+1,h}|O),(\mu|E_{h,h+1}),(\mu|E_{h+1,h})\}
(See Lemmas
4.1
and
4.3
below), then the matrices
{M^{\!\star}}
, appearing in (
2.1.2
), or in the leading term if
{B^{\!\star}}
is odd, take the
form

({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}),({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}),({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})
etc.
Note that the base matrices of
{M^{\!\star}}
are all of the
form

A^{+}_{h,k}
or
A^{-}_{h,k}
.

We end this preliminary section with the definition of the superalgebras
{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
of superhomomorphisms.
More precisely,
given
{R}
-free supermodule
4
4

4

By an
R
-free supermodule, we mean an
R
-free module with a basis consisting of homogeneous elements.

V,W
,
we used
\mathrm{Hom}_{{R}}(V,W)
to denote the set of all
{R}
-linear maps from
V
to
W
.
We also made
\mathrm{Hom}_{{R}}(V,W)
into an
R
-free supermodule by declaring

{\mathrm{Hom}_{{R}}(V,W)}_{\overline{i}}

to be the set of homogeneous map of parity
\bar{i}\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{2}
,
that is,
the linear maps
\theta:V\rightarrow W
with
\theta(V_{\overline{j}})\subset W_{\overline{i}+\overline{j}}
for
\bar{j}\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{2}
.

If
R
is also a superalgbera, we may define
\mathrm{Hom}^{s}_{{R}}(V,W)
to be the set of all
R
-superhomomorphisms
f:V\to W
such that

f(rv)=(-1)^{\wp({f})\wp({r})}rf(v),\;\;\text{ for all }r\in R,v\in V.

Note that it is typical in a category of super objects to write the expressions
which only make sense for homogeneous elements,
and the expected meaning for arbitrary elements
is obtained by extending linearly from the homogeneous case.

Thus, following
[
CW
, Definition 1.1]
,
we define, for right
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
-modules
M,N
,

\mathrm{Hom}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M,N)
to be the
R
-supermodule generated by
all
\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
-superhomomorphisms
\Phi:M\to N
satisfying

(2.3.1)

\Phi(mh)=(-1)^{\wp({\Phi})\wp({h})}\Phi(m)h\;\;(m\in M,h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}).

In particular, for
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
,
let
\mathrm{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M):=\mathrm{Hom}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M,M)
.
One checks easily that, if

\Phi\in\mathrm{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M)_{\bar{i}}
and
\Psi\in\mathrm{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M)_{\bar{j}}
, then
\Psi\Phi\in\mathrm{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(M)_{\bar{i}+\bar{j}}
. Thus, we obtain a new (associative) superalgebra

(2.3.2)

{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}:=\mathrm{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\Big)=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)}\mathrm{Hom}^{s}_{\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}}(x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R},x_{\mu}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}).

This is called the
standardised queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
.

The following result shows that the standardised queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
is just a sign modification of the original queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
.

Proposition 2.4
.

The standardised queer
q
-Schur superalgebra
{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
is
R
-free with basis
\{\Phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}\ \bm{|}\ {A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}\}
, where
\Phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}:\oplus_{\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\mu}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\rightarrow\oplus_{\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\mu}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
is defined by

(2.4.1)

\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}(x_{\mu}h)={(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({h})}\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}\Phi_{{A^{\!\star}}}(x_{\mathrm{co}(A)})h={(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({h})}\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}T_{A^{\!\star}}h,

for all
\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
and
h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
.

Moreover,
for any
{A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with
\mathrm{ro}(A)=\mathrm{co}(B)
,
if
\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}
and
\Phi_{B^{\!\star}}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}\Phi_{M^{\!\star}}
with structure respective constants

{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}},\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}\in R
, then

(2.4.2)

{\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}={(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})}\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}=\begin{cases}-\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}},&\text{ if both ${A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}$ are odd};\\
\;\;\,\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}}

Proof.

The freeness follows easily from the linear isomorphism

(2.4.3)

(\;\;)^{\dagger}:\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)\to{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}

as defined in
[
CW
, (1.1)]
or, more precisely, for a homogeneous
\phi\in\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
of parity
\wp({\phi})
and
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}
,
\phi^{\dagger}:M\to M
is defined by

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle\phi^{{\dagger}}(m)=(-1)^{\wp({\phi})\cdot\wp({m})}\phi(m)

for homogeneous
m\in M
. It is straightforward to check
\phi^{{\dagger}}\in{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
and
\dagger\dagger=1
. The basis assertion is clear by setting
\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\phi_{A^{\!\star}}^{{\dagger}}
for
all
{A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
.

Finally,
if we write
T_{A^{\!\star}}=x_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}h^{\prime}
,
where
h^{\prime}={T_{d_{A}}}c_{{A^{\!\star}}}\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{D}_{{\nu}_{{}_{A}}}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{co}(A)}}T_{\sigma}
(see (
2.0.12
)),
then
h^{\prime}
is homogeneous with parity
\wp(h^{\prime})=\wp({A^{\!\star}})

and the structure constants
{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}

appearing in
\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}

are determined by writing
T_{B^{\!\star}}h^{\prime}\in x_{\mathrm{ro}(B)}\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}\cap\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}x_{\mathrm{co}(A)}

as a linear combination of
T_{M^{\!\star}}
’s. In other words,

(2.4.5)

z({B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}):=\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}(x_{\mathrm{co}(A)})=T_{B^{\!\star}}h^{\prime}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}T_{M^{\!\star}},

and, for each
{M^{\!\star}}
, we have
\wp({{M^{\!\star}}})=\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})+\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})
.

Thus,
for any homogeneous element
h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}

and
\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
, by (
2.4.1
),
we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{B^{\!\star}}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}(x_{\mu}h)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}{(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({h})}{(-1)}^{\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})\cdot(\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})+\wp({h}))}\phi_{B^{\!\star}}(T_{{A^{\!\star}}}h)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}{(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({h})}{(-1)}^{\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})\cdot(\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})+\wp({h}))}\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}T_{M^{\!\star}}\cdot h

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}{(-1)}^{\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})}\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{(-1)}^{\wp({{M^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({h})}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}(x_{\mu})\cdot h

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}{(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})}\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\Phi_{M^{\!\star}}(x_{\mu}h).

Hence,
{\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}={(-1)}^{\wp({{A^{\!\star}}})\cdot\wp({{B^{\!\star}}})}\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}
,
as desired.
∎

We have seen above that the standardised version
{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
is linearly isomorphic to
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
as free
R
-modules. The next result shows that if, in addition,
\sqrt{-1}\in R
, then
{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)
as
R
-superalgebras.

Corollary 2.5
.

If
\sqrt{-1}\in R
,
then the
R
-linear isomorphism
(\;\;)^{\dagger}
in (
2.4.3
) induces an
isomorphism of
R
-superalgebras

{\mathfrak{\tau}}:\mathcal{Q}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)},\;\;{\phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}}\longmapsto{(\sqrt{-1})}^{\wp({\phi_{A^{\!\star}}})}{\Phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}},\;\;\forall{A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}.

Proof.

Clearly,
{\mathfrak{\tau}}
is an
R
-linear isomorphism.
It remains to check
{\mathfrak{\tau}}({\phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}}{\phi}_{{B^{\!\star}}})={\mathfrak{\tau}}({\phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}}){\mathfrak{\tau}}({\phi}_{{B^{\!\star}}})

for all
{A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
. By writing
\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}
, as in Lemma
2.4
,
this can be easily proved by the following facts:

(a)
{(\sqrt{-1})}^{\wp({\phi_{A^{\!\star}}})}{(\sqrt{-1})}^{\wp({\phi_{B^{\!\star}}})}{(-1)}^{\wp({\phi_{A^{\!\star}}})\wp({\phi_{B^{\!\star}}})}={(\sqrt{-1})}^{\wp({{\phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}}{\phi}_{{B^{\!\star}}}})}
;

(b)
\wp({{\phi}_{{B^{\!\star}}}{\phi}_{{A^{\!\star}}}})=\wp({{\phi}_{{M^{\!\star}}}})
for each
{M^{\!\star}}
with
\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}\neq 0
.
∎

3.
The quantum queer supergroup and standard basis for
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)

We first introduced the quantum queer supergroups
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
and their associated Schur–Weyl duality (see
[
Ol
]
). Then we investigate the standard basis, as a normalization of the natural basis, for the generic superalgebra
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
over
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}[{\upsilon},{\upsilon}^{-1}]
.

The following definition of
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
, introduced by Olshanski
[
Ol
]
, is taken from

[
DW1
, Prop. 5.2]
(cf.
[
GJKK
, §2]
).
Recall the standard basis
\{\epsilon_{i}\ \bm{|}\ i\in[1,n]\}
for
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and define the “dot product”
\epsilon_{i}\centerdot\epsilon_{j}=\delta_{i,j}
, linearly extended to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
.

Definition 3.1
.

The quantum queer supergroup
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
(
n\geq 2
) is the (Hopf) superalgebra
over
\mathbb{Q}({v})
generated by
even generators
{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}
,
{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}
,
{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}
,
{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}
,
and odd generators
{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}
,
{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}
,
{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}
,
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
, subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle({\rm QQ1})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}=1,\qquad{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},\qquad{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}=2{\delta}_{i,j}\frac{{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{2}-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-2}}{{v}^{2}-{v}^{-2}};

\displaystyle({\rm QQ2})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}={v}^{\epsilon_{i}\centerdot\alpha_{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},\qquad{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}={v}^{\epsilon_{i}\centerdot\alpha_{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}={v}^{-(\epsilon_{i}\centerdot\alpha_{j})}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},\qquad{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}={v}^{-(\epsilon_{i}\centerdot\alpha_{j})}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i};\;(\mbox{here }\alpha_{j}=\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{j+1})

\displaystyle({\rm QQ3})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}-{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1},\qquad{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i-1}-{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}=-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i-1}},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}-{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}=-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},\qquad{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i-1}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i-1}},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}+{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1},\qquad{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i-1}}+{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i-1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i-1},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}+{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i},\qquad{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i-1}}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i-1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i-1},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}=0,\mbox{ for }j\neq i,i-1;

\displaystyle({\rm QQ4})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}^{-1}-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}}{{v}-{v}^{-1}},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}=\delta_{i,j}(\frac{{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}^{-1}}{{v}-{v}^{-1}}+({v}-{v}^{-1}){{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i+1}}),

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}=\delta_{i,j}({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{-1}),\qquad{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}=\delta_{i,j}({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i+1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i}}-{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i});

\displaystyle({\rm QQ5})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}^{2}=-\frac{{v}-{v}^{-1}}{{v}+{v}^{-1}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}^{2},\quad{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}^{2}=\frac{{v}-{v}^{-1}}{{v}+{v}^{-1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}-{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}=0,\quad\mbox{ for }|i-j|\neq 1,

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}={{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}=0\quad\mbox{ for }\quad|i-j|>1,

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i+1}-{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i+1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i+1}}+{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}},\qquad{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i+1}}-{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i+1}-{v}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i+1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{i}},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i+1}-{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i+1}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}=-({{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i+1}}+{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}),\qquad{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i+1}}-{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i+1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}={{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i+1}-{v}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i+1}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{i}};

\displaystyle({\rm QQ6})\quad

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}^{2}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}-({v}+{v}^{-1}){{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}+{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}^{2}=0,\qquad{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}^{2}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-({v}+{v}^{-1}){{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}^{2}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}-({v}+{v}^{-1}){{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}+{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{i}^{2}=0,\qquad{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}^{2}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}-({v}+{v}^{-1}){{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{i}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{ where }\quad|i-j|=1.

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that the relations in (QQ3) imply

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{E}}}_{\overline{j}}

\displaystyle=-{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}+{v}^{-1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}},

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}

\displaystyle={v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{v}^{-1}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}},

and the third relation in (QQ4) implies

\displaystyle{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j}}

\displaystyle={{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}-{{\mathsf{F}}}_{\overline{j}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}+{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}

\displaystyle={{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}-{v}^{-1}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}-{v}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}+{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j}+{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j}^{-1}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{j+1}}{{\mathsf{K}}}_{j+1}.

Hence, given
{\mathsf{K}}_{\bar{n}}
and by induction on
j
in descending order from
n-1
to
1
,
one can obtain all other odd generators in
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
. In other words,

{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
can be generated by
\{{{\mathsf{E}}}_{j},{{\mathsf{F}}}_{j},{{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}^{\pm 1},{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{n}}\ \bm{|}\ 1\leq j\leq n-1,\ 1\leq i\leq n\}
; see
[
GJKKK
]
(cf.,
[
DLZ
, Rem. 2.2]
).
This fact shows that it suffices to use the even generators together with
{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\overline{n}}
to describe the multiplication formulas for the regular representation.

The supergroup
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
and Hecke-Clifford algebra
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}^{c}_{r}
enjoys a beautiful duality. This involves the queer
q
-Schur superalgebras introduced in the previous section.

From now on, we assume the ground ring
R={\mathcal{Z}}(:=\mathbb{Z}[{\upsilon},{\upsilon}^{-1}])
or
R=\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})
and define the following standardised queer
{\upsilon}
-Schur superalgebras:

(3.2.1)

\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r):=\mathcal{Q}_{q}^{s}(n,r;\mathcal{Z}),\qquad\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r):=\mathcal{Q}_{q}^{s}(n,r;\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon}))\;\;(q={\upsilon}^{2}).

Following
[
Ol
]
,
there is a Schur-Weyl type duality
between
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
and
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
on the tensor space
{\bm{V}}(n|n)^{\otimes r}
, where

{\bm{V}}(n|n):=V(n|n)_{\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})}
is the superspace of both even and odd dimension
n
.
Meanwhile by
[
DW2
, Cor. 6.3]
, the tensor space
\bm{V}(n|n)^{\otimes r}

can be identified with
\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)}x_{\mu}\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
as
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
-supermdoules.
The following result is taken from
[
Ol
, Th. 5.3]
(with
\mathbb{C}
replaced by
\mathbb{Q}
) and
[
DW1
, Th. 9.2]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

There is a superalgebra epimorphisms
5
5

5

The homomorphism considered in
[
DW2
, Prop. 6.4]
is a composition of
\Phi_{r}
, base change to
\mathbb{C}({\upsilon})
, with the isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r;\mathbb{C}({\upsilon}))\cong\mathcal{Q}_{\upsilon}(n,r;\mathbb{C}({\upsilon}))
given by Corollary
2.5
.

{\Phi}_{r}:{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})\to\text{\rm End}^{s}_{\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}^{c}_{r}}\big(\bm{V}(n|n)^{\otimes r}\big)
and

{\Psi}_{r}:\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}\to\text{\rm End}^{s}_{{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})}\big(\bm{V}(n|n)^{\otimes r}\big)
. Moreover,
{\rm{ker}}({\Phi}_{r})
is generated by the elements

(3.3.1)

{{\mathsf{K}}}_{1}\cdots{{\mathsf{K}}}_{n}-{\upsilon}^{r},\;\;({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-1)({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-{\upsilon})\cdots({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-{\upsilon}^{r}),\;\;{{\mathsf{K}}}_{\bar{i}}({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-{\upsilon})({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-{\upsilon}^{2})\cdots({{\mathsf{K}}}_{i}-{\upsilon}^{r}),

for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Note that
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)\cong\text{\rm End}^{s}_{\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}^{c}_{r}}\big(\bm{V}(n|n)^{\otimes r}\big)
. We identify the two in the sequel.
A more structure-preserving
\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})
-superalgebra homomorphism
\bm{\xi}_{n,r}
from
{\bm{U}}_{\!{v}}(\mathfrak{{q}}_{n})
to
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
will be constructed in §6. For this purpose, we need a process of standardisation of the natural basis and fundamental multiplication formulas in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
.

We first standardise the defining basis for the generic Hecke-Clifford superalgebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{r}^{c}
over
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\bm{{\mathcal{H}}}_{r}^{c}
over
\mathbb{Q}({\upsilon})
.
For any
i\in[1,r-1]
, set
\mathcal{T}_{i}={\upsilon}^{-1}T_{i}
as usual, then the new generators
\mathcal{T}_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{r}^{c}
satisfy the following relations (cf. (
2.0.1
)):

(3.3.2)

\begin{aligned} &(\mathcal{T}_{i}-{\upsilon})(\mathcal{T}_{i}+{\upsilon}^{-1})=0,\quad\\
&\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{j}=\mathcal{T}_{j}\mathcal{T}_{i}\,\;(|i-j|>1),\quad\\
&\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i+1}\mathcal{T}_{i}=\mathcal{T}_{i+1}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i+1}\;(i\neq r),\end{aligned}\quad\mathcal{T}_{i}c_{j}=\begin{cases}c_{j}\mathcal{T}_{i}\,&\text{ if }j\neq i,i+1;\\
c_{i+1}\mathcal{T}_{i},&\text{ if }j=i;\\
c_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}-({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1})(c_{i}-c_{i+1}),&\text{ if }j=i+1.\end{cases}

We now standardise the natural basis
\{\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}\}_{A^{\!\star}}
defined in Proposition
2.4
. For any subset
D\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{r}
, let

T_{D}=\sum_{w\in D}T_{w}
.

Recall from the definition of
T_{A^{\!\star}}
in (
2.0.12
). It is obtained from the even part

T_{A}:=T_{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}d_{A}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}}=x_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}T_{d_{A}}\Sigma_{A}

by inserting the
c
-element
c_{A^{\!\star}}
between
d_{A}
and
\Sigma_{A}
, where
\Sigma_{A}=T_{\mathcal{D}_{{\nu}_{A}}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{co}(A)}}
denotes the “tail term”.
Motivated by the standard basis element
\xi_{A}
for
q
-Schur algebra
S_{q}(n,r)
in
[
DDPW
, (9.3.1)]
, we may standardise the even part
T_{A}
, by setting

(3.3.3)

\mathcal{T}_{A}={\upsilon}^{-\ell(d_{A}^{+})}T_{A},

where

d^{+}_{A}
is the longest element in
{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}d_{A}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{ro}(A)}}
.

We need to define the standardisation of
c_{A^{\!\star}}
. Recall
the
c
-elements
c_{{q},i,j}
and
c^{\prime}_{{q},i,j}
in
[
DW2
, Section 4]
:

\displaystyle c_{{q},i,j}

\displaystyle={q}^{j-i}c_{i}+\cdots+{q}c_{j-1}+c_{j}={\upsilon}^{j-i}({{\upsilon}}^{j-i}c_{i}+\cdots+{{\upsilon}^{-(j-i)+2}}c_{j-1}+{{\upsilon}}^{-(j-i)}c_{j}),

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{{q},i,j}

\displaystyle=c_{i}+{q}c_{i+1}+\cdots+{q}^{j-i}c_{j}={\upsilon}^{j-i}({{\upsilon}}^{-(j-i)}c_{i}+\cdots+{{\upsilon}^{(j-i)-2}}c_{j-1}+{{\upsilon}}^{(j-i)}c_{j}).

Since
q={\upsilon}^{2}
, we may introduce the standard version
\mathfrak{o}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j}
and
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j}
such that

c_{{q},i,j}={\upsilon}^{j-i}\mathfrak{o}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j},\qquad c^{\prime}_{{q},i,j}={\upsilon}^{j-i}\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j}.

In other words, we have

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j}

\displaystyle={{\upsilon}}^{j-i}c_{i}+\cdots+{{\upsilon}^{-(j-i)+2}}c_{j-1}+{{\upsilon}}^{-(j-i)}c_{j},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}_{{{\upsilon}},i,j}

\displaystyle={{\upsilon}}^{-(j-i)}c_{i}+\cdots+{{\upsilon}^{(j-i)-2}}c_{j-1}+{{\upsilon}}^{(j-i)}c_{j},

Thus, for any
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}})=(a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}|a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
, substituting every
c_{q,i,j}
in
c_{{A^{\!\star}}}
by
{\upsilon}^{(j-i)}\mathfrak{o}_{{\upsilon},i,j}
, we obtain
c_{{A^{\!\star}}}={\upsilon}^{\sum_{i,j}a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}}\mathfrak{o}_{{A^{\!\star}}}.

Putting
A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}}:=\sum_{i,j}a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}
(a “dot product”), we obtain the standard form of
c_{A^{\!\star}}
:

(3.3.5)

\mathfrak{o}_{{A^{\!\star}}}={\upsilon}^{-A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}}}c_{A^{\!\star}}.

Combining the two standardisations (
3.3.3
) and (
3.3.5
) gives the following standard form of
T_{A^{\!\star}}
:

\mathcal{T}_{{A^{\!\star}}}={\upsilon}^{-\ell(d^{+}_{A})-{A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}}}}T_{A^{\!\star}}.

In particular, if
A=(\mu|O)
for some
\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
, then
\mathcal{T}_{(\mu|O)}={\upsilon}^{-\ell(w_{0,\mu})}x_{\mu}
, where
w_{0,\mu}
is the longest element in
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
.

Definition 3.4
.

For
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}})=(a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}|a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
, define

[{A^{\!\star}}]\in{\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{{q}}({n},{r};R)}
such that

(3.4.1)

[{A^{\!\star}}](\mathcal{T}_{(\mu|O)}h)=\delta_{\mathrm{co}(A),\mu}(-1)^{\wp({A^{\!\star}})\wp(h)}\mathcal{T}_{{A^{\!\star}}}h,\forall h\in\mathcal{H}^{c}_{r,R}.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
{A^{\!\star}}=(a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}|a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with base
A=(a_{i,j})
, let

(3.5.1)

\partial({A^{\!\star}}):=\sum_{i\geq k,j<l}a_{i,j}a_{k,l}+\sum_{i,j}a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}.

Then

[{A^{\!\star}}]={\upsilon}^{-\partial({A^{\!\star}})}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}
.

See
[
DDPW
, (13.2.5)]
for a geometric interpretation of the number

\sum_{i\geq k,j<l}a_{i,j}a_{k,l}
.

Proof.

By definition (see Proposition
2.4
), it is clear (cf.
[
DDPW
, (9.3.1)]
) that

[{A^{\!\star}}]={\upsilon}^{-\ell(d^{+}_{A})+\ell(w_{0,\mathrm{co}(A)})-A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}}}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}.

On the other hand,
[
DDPW
, (13.2.5), Lem. 13.10]
implies

(3.5.2)

\widehat{\ell}(A):=\ell(d^{+}_{A})-\ell(w_{0,\mathrm{co}(A)})=\sum_{i\geq k,j<l}a_{i,j}a_{k,l}=\partial({A^{\!\star}})-A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}},

proving the assertion.
∎

By Propositions
3.5
and
2.4
, we have immediately the following.

Corollary 3.6
.

The set

\{[{A^{\!\star}}]\ \bm{|}\ {A^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}\}
forms a basis for the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-superalgebra
\mathcal{Q}_{\upsilon}^{s}(n,r)
.

This basis is called the
standard (integral) basis
for
\mathcal{Q}_{\upsilon}^{s}(n,r)
. In the next section, we will turn the fundamental multiplication formulas into standard multiplication formulas, especially for those discussed in Remark
2.3
. The following result will simplify the computation for the number
\partial({M^{\!\star}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})
, where
{M^{\!\star}}
is the matrix mentioned in Remark
2.3
with base
A^{+}_{h,k}
or
A^{-}_{h,k}
.

For any
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
, let

(3.6.1)

{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}:={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}(A)=\sum_{j>k}a_{h,j},\qquad{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}:={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}(A)=\sum_{j<k}a_{h,j}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Maintain the notation above. We have

\widehat{\ell}(A^{+}_{h,k})-\widehat{\ell}(A)={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1},\quad\widehat{\ell}(A^{-}_{h,k})-\widehat{\ell}(A)=-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}.

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime}=A^{+}_{h,k}=A+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}=(a_{i,j}^{\prime})
. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}(A^{+}_{h,k})-\widehat{\ell}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq k,j<l}a^{\prime}_{i,j}a^{\prime}_{k,l}-\sum_{i\geq k,j<l}a_{i,j}a_{k,l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq h,j<k}a_{i,j}-\sum_{i\geq h+1,j<k}a_{i,j}+\sum_{i\leq h,j>k}a_{i,j}-\sum_{i\leq h+1,j>k}a_{i,j}={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1},

as desired. The proof for the other case is similar.
∎

4.
Standard multiplication formulas in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)

In this section, we standardise the fundamental multiplication formulas for
\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}
arising from
[
DGLW
, §§5-7]
via Proposition
2.4
. In other words, we derive multiplication formulas in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
relative to the standard basis
\{[{A^{\!\star}}]\}_{{A^{\!\star}}}
introduced in the previous section by modifying those in
[
DGLW
]
.

The modification process is in two steps. First, we use Proposition
2.4
to turn a formula of the form
\phi_{B^{\!\star}}\cdot\phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\gamma^{{M^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}}}}\phi_{M^{\!\star}}
in the natural basis
\{\phi_{A^{\!\star}}\}_{{A^{\!\star}}}
to a formula of the form
\Phi_{B^{\!\star}}\cdot\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}}{\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}\Phi_{M^{\!\star}}
in the superhomomorphism basis
\{\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}\}_{{A^{\!\star}}}
, the natural basis for
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
. Second, compute the number

\partial({M^{\!\star}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})-\partial({B^{\!\star}})
in
the structure constants
{{\textsc{p}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}:={\upsilon}^{\partial({M^{\!\star}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})-\partial({B^{\!\star}})}\textsc{c}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}
relative to the standard basis
\{[{A^{\!\star}}]\}_{{A^{\!\star}}}
. (In other words, the first step determines the sign and the second step determines the power of
{\upsilon}
.)
The standard multiplication formulas have the form

(4.0.1)

[{B^{\!\star}}]\cdot[{A^{\!\star}}]=\sum_{{M^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}}{{\textsc{p}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}[{M^{\!\star}}].

As usual, for notational simplicity, we make the following convention: if a term
{{\textsc{p}}_{{B^{\!\star}},{A^{\!\star}},{M^{\!\star}}}}[{M^{\!\star}}]
with
{M^{\!\star}}\notin M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
appears in a multiplication formula in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
, then we regard the entire term as 0.

Keeping the notations in
[
DGLW
]
, we also regard any
\lambda\in\Lambda(n,r)

as a diagonal matrix
when writing
\lambda+B:={{\rm{diag}}}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})+B
, for
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
.

We first observe that, for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
q={\upsilon}^{2}
,

(4.0.2)

[\![a+1]\!]_{q,q^{2}}:=[\![a+1]\!]_{q}-[\![a+1]\!]_{q^{2}}=-({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1}){\upsilon}^{2a+1}\left[a+1\atop 2\right].

This polynomial occurs in some fundamental multiplication formulas in
[
DGLW
]
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
, let
{B^{\!\star}}
in (
4.0.1
) be one of the following even matrices

(4.0.3)

D^{\star}_{\mu}:=(\mu|O),\;\;E^{\star}_{h,\lambda}:=(\lambda+E_{h,h+1}-E_{h+1,h+1}|O),\;\;F^{\star}_{h,\lambda}:=(\lambda-E_{h,h}+E_{h+1,h}|O).

Following the two step modification process, we obtain three standard multiplication formulas in this even case.
For notational simplicity, we also introduce the following, using (
3.6.1
),

(4.0.4)

f_{h}(A,k)={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}({A})-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}({A});\qquad g_{h}(A,k)=-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}({A})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}({A}).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
h\in[1,n-1]
and
{A^{\!\star}}=({{a}^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}}|{{a}^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
.
Then, for
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(n,r)
, the following multiplication formulas hold in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
:

\displaystyle(1)\;\;\;\;[D^{\star}_{\mu}][{A^{\!\star}}]=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{ro}(A)}[{A^{\!\star}}],\qquad[{A^{\!\star}}][D^{\star}_{\mu}]=\delta_{\mu,\mathrm{co}(A)}[{A^{\!\star}}].

\displaystyle(2)\;\;{[E^{\star}_{h,\lambda}][{A^{\!\star}}]}=

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda,\mathrm{ro}(A)}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\upsilon}^{f_{h}(A,k)}\Big\{{\upsilon}^{{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1][{A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}]

\displaystyle+{\upsilon}^{-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}}[{A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}]

\displaystyle-({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1}){\upsilon}^{-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}}\left[{a_{h,k}+1}\atop 2\right][{A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}]\Big\}.

\displaystyle(3)\;\;{[F^{\star}_{h,\lambda}][{A^{\!\star}}]}=

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda,\mathrm{ro}(A)}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\upsilon}^{g_{h}(A,k)}\Big\{{\upsilon}^{-a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}+1][{A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}+E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}]

\displaystyle+{{\upsilon}}^{a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}}[{A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}+E_{h+1,k}]

\displaystyle-({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1}){{\upsilon}}^{a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}}\left[{a_{h+1,k}+1}\atop 2\right][{A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}]\Big\}.

Proof.

In all three cases, the even parity of
{B^{\!\star}}
implies that the sign in (
2.4.2
) can be dropped.
Thus, the formula in (1) follows easily from
[
DGLW
, Th. 5.3(1)]
.

For (2),
by
[
DGLW
, Th. 5.3(2)]
and Proposition
2.4
, we have for
{\lambda}=\mathrm{ro}(A)

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{E^{\star}_{h,\lambda}}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big\{{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}}{[\![{{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1}]\!]}_{{q}}\Phi_{({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})}

\displaystyle+{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}}\Phi_{({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})}+{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-1}{[\![{a_{h,k}+1}]\!]}_{{q},{q}^{2}}\Phi_{({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})}\Big\}.

Thus, in this case, the matrices
{M^{\!\star}}
in (
4.0.1
) have the form

{M^{\!\star}}=\begin{cases}({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}),&\text{ if }a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}>0;\\
({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}),&\text{ if }a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=0,a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}=1;\\
({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}),&\text{ if }a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=1,a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}=1.\end{cases}

To compute the number
\partial({M^{\!\star}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})-\partial({B^{\!\star}})
, by (
3.5.1
) and Lemma
3.7
,

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle\partial(E^{\star}_{h,\lambda})=\lambda_{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{h,j}=:{\bf r}_{h},

\displaystyle\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})=\widehat{\ell}(A^{+}_{h,k})-\widehat{\ell}(A)+A^{\overline{0},+}_{h,k}*A^{\overline{1}}-A^{\overline{0}}*A^{\overline{1}}

\displaystyle={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}+a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}.

Observing that
({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})\mbox{ and }({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})
have the same base matrix
({A}^{\bar{0}}+{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})
as
({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})
, applying (
4.1.2
) leads to

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})=\partial({A^{\!\star}})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}+a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k},

\displaystyle\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})=\partial({A^{\!\star}})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}.

Continuing the computations on the coefficients, for
{M^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}})
, the coefficient in (
4.1.1
) has the form (
q={\upsilon}^{2}
):

{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}}{[\![{{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1}]\!]}_{{q}}={\upsilon}^{2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+2{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}+{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1].

By (
4.1.2
), we obtain the power of
{\upsilon}

(4.1.4)

\displaystyle 2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+2{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}+{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+\partial({M^{\!\star}})-(\partial(E^{\star}_{h,\lambda})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle=2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+2{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}+{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+\partial({A^{\!\star}})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}+a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}-({\bf r}_{h}+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}+{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}=f_{h}(A,k)+{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}.

For
{M^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with
a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=0
and
a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}=1
.
By (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.3
), the power of
{\upsilon}
becomes

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle 2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+\partial({M^{\!\star}})-(\partial(E^{\star}_{h,\lambda})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle=2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+\partial({A^{\!\star}})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}+a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}-(\partial(E^{\star}_{h,\lambda})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}=f_{h}(A,k)-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}.

Finally, for
{M^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with
a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}=1
,
We have, by (
4.0.2
),

{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-1}{[\![{a_{h,k}+1}]\!]}_{{q},{q}^{2}}=-{\upsilon}^{2({\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-1)+2a_{h,k}+1}({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1})\left[{a_{h,k}+1}\atop 2\right].

The power of
{\upsilon}
has the form, by (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.3
),

(4.1.6)

\displaystyle 2({\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-1)+2a_{h,k}+1+\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k})-(\partial(E^{\star}_{h,\lambda})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle=2({\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-1)+2a_{h,k}+1+\partial({A^{\!\star}})+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}-{\bf r}_{h}-\partial({A^{\!\star}})

\displaystyle={{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}}=f_{h}(A,k)-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}.

Substituting all three cases into (
4.1.1
) yields (2).
Formula (3) can be proved similarly.∎

We now derive the standard multiplication formulas (
4.0.1
) with
{B^{\!\star}}
being one of the following three matrices with odd parity:

({\lambda}|E_{h,h}),\qquad(\mu|E_{h,h+1}),\qquad(\nu|E_{h+1,h}).

Recall the preorder
\preceq
over
M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
from
[
BLM
]
and its associated equivalence relation
\approx
: for
A=(a_{i,j}),B=(b_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N})
,

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle B\preceq A

\displaystyle\iff\begin{cases}\sum_{i\leq s,j\geq t}b_{i,j}\leq\sum_{i\leq s,j\geq t}a_{i,j},&\mbox{ for all }s<t;\\
\sum_{i\geq s,j\leq t}b_{i,j}\leq\sum_{i\geq s,j\leq t}a_{i,j},&\mbox{ for all }s>t.\end{cases}

\displaystyle B\approx A

\displaystyle\iff B\preceq A,\;\;A\preceq B.

Then,

B\approx A
if and only if
B^{\pm}=A^{\pm}
, where
X^{\pm}
is the matrix obtained from
X
with all diagonal entries replaced by 0s. We also write
B\prec A
for
B\preceq A
but
B^{\pm}\neq A^{\pm}
.

Similar to (
4.0.3
), we introduce the following matrices:

(4.1.8)

D^{\star}_{\overline{i},\lambda}:=(\lambda-E_{i,i}|E_{i,i}),\;\;E^{\star}_{\overline{h},\lambda}:=(\lambda-E_{h+1,h+1}|E_{h,h+1}),\;\;F^{\star}_{\overline{h},\lambda}:=(\lambda-E_{h,h}|E_{h+1,h}).

We also set (cf. (
4.0.4
)), for
A
as above and
h,k\in[1,n]
,

(4.1.9)

d_{h}(A,k):={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}(A)-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}(A).

The following is the standardisation of
[
DGLW
, Th. 6.2]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}})=(a^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}|a^{\bar{1}}_{i,j})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with base
A=A^{\bar{0}}+A^{\bar{1}},

\lambda=\mathrm{ro}(A)
and
h\in[1,n]
.

(1)

If
A
satisfies the SDP condition on the
h
-th row,
then we have in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)
:

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle[{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}]}[{A^{\!\star}}]}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}{(-1)}^{\wp({A^{\!\star}})+{\widetilde{a}}^{\overline{1}}_{h,k}}{{\upsilon}}^{d_{h}(A,k)}\Big\{[{{A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}}]+[a_{h,k}]_{{\upsilon}^{2}}[{{A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}}]\Big\}

\displaystyle=:{}_{\text{\sc sdp}}{\rm{H}}\overline{\textsc{k}}(h,{A^{\!\star}}).

(2)

In general, we have

{{[{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}]}[{A^{\!\star}}]}=\begin{cases}{}_{\text{\sc sdp}}{\rm{H}}\overline{\textsc{k}}(h,{A^{\!\star}})+\sum_{{B^{\!\star}},{B}\prec{{A}}}{{\textsc{p}}_{{{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}},{A^{\!\star}}},{B^{\!\star}}}}[{{B^{\!\star}}}],&\text{ if }h<n;\\
{}_{\text{\sc sdp}}{\rm{H}}\overline{\textsc{k}}(n,{A^{\!\star}}),&\text{ if }h=n,\end{cases}

where
{B^{\!\star}}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
and
{{\textsc{p}}_{{{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}},{A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Proof.

We first prove (1). Since
\wp({{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}})=1
, by

[
DGLW
, Th. 6.2]
(1) and Proposition
2.4
,

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle[{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}]}[{A^{\!\star}}]}={\upsilon}^{-\partial(D^{\star}_{\bar{h}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})}\Phi_{D^{\star}_{\bar{h}}}\Phi_{A^{\!\star}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\wp({A^{\!\star}})}{\upsilon}^{-\partial(D^{\star}_{\bar{h}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{(-1)}^{{{\widetilde{a}}^{\bar{1}}}_{h,k}}{q}^{{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}}\Big\{\Phi_{({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k})}+{{[\![{a_{h,k}}]\!]}_{{q}^{2}}}\Phi_{({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k})}\Big\}.

We want to write this as a linear combination of
[{M^{\!\star}}]
as in (
4.0.1
). First, we see from
(
3.5.1
)

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle\partial({{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}})=\lambda_{h}-1=\mathrm{ro}(A)_{h}-1={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+a_{h,k}-1,

\displaystyle\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k})=\partial({A^{\!\star}})+a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-1,

\displaystyle\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k})=\partial({A^{\!\star}})-a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}.

For
{M^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k})
(forcing
a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=0
), (
4.2.2
) implies

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle 2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k})-(\partial({{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle=2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+\partial({A^{\!\star}})+a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-1-({\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+a_{h,k}-1)-\partial({A^{\!\star}})

\displaystyle={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}=d_{h}(A,k).

For
{M^{\!\star}}=({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k})
(forcing
a^{\bar{1}}_{h,k}=1
), since
{{[\![{a_{h,k}}]\!]}_{{q}^{2}}}={\upsilon}^{2(a_{h,k}-1)}[a_{h,k}]_{{\upsilon}^{2}}
, we have by (
4.2.2
),

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle 2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+2(a_{h,k}-1)+\partial({A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k})-\partial({{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}})+\partial({A^{\!\star}}))

\displaystyle=2{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+2(a_{h,k}-1)+\partial({A^{\!\star}})-a^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}-({\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+a_{h,k}-1)-\partial({A^{\!\star}})

\displaystyle={\overrightarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}=d_{h}(A,k).

Substituting (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.4
) into (
4.2.1
) yields (1).

We now prove (2). The case when
h=n
follows from the last assertion of Theorem
2.2
and (1). The general case when
h<n
follows from
[
DGLW
, Th. 6.2(2)]
by setting

{{\textsc{p}}_{{{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}},{A^{\!\star}},{B^{\!\star}}}}=(-1)^{\wp({A^{\!\star}})}{\upsilon}^{\partial({B^{\!\star}})-\partial({{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}})-\partial({A^{\!\star}})}{f}^{{{D^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}},{A^{\!\star}}}_{{B^{\!\star}}}
.
∎

The next result is the standardisation of
[
DGLW
, Ths. 6.3–4]
. In addition to the notation
g_{h}(A,k)=-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}+{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1}
defined in (
4.0.4
), we also introduce the new notation

(4.2.5)

g^{-}_{h}(A,k)=-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h}-{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{h+1},\;\;\text{ where}\;\;{\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{j}={\overleftarrow{\bf r}}^{k}_{j}(A).

Lemma 4.3
.

For
h\in[1,n-1]
and
{A^{\!\star}}=(A^{\bar{0}}|A^{\bar{1}})=({{a}^{\bar{0}}_{i,j}}|{{a}^{\bar{1}}_{i,j}})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{N}|{{\mathbb{N}}}_{2})_{r}
with base
A=A^{\bar{0}}+A^{\bar{1}}

and
\lambda=\mathrm{ro}(A)
.

(1)

Suppose that, for every
k\in[1,n]
such that
a_{h+1,k}>0
and
A
satisfies the SDP condition at
(h,k)
if
a_{h,k}>0
and
A_{\text{\normalsize$\urcorner$}}^{h,k}=0
if
a_{h,k}=0
.
Then
we have in
\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{\upsilon}(n,r)

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle{{[{E^{\star}_{\bar{h},\lambda}}]}[{A^{\!\star}}]}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\wp({A^{\!\star}})}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\upsilon}^{f_{h}(A,k)}\Big\{{(-1)}^{{{\widetilde{a}}^{\bar{1}}}_{h-1,k}}{\upsilon}^{a^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}}[{{A}^{\bar{0}}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}+E_{h,k}}]

\displaystyle+{(-1)}^{{{\widetilde{a}}^{\bar{1}}}_{h,k}+1}{\upsilon}^{-a^{\bar{0}}_{h+1,k}}[{a}^{\bar{0}}_{h,k}+1][{{A}^{\bar{0}}+E_{h,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h+1,k}}]

\displaystyle+{(-1)}^{{{\widetilde{a}}^{\bar{1}}}_{h-1,k}}{\upsilon}^{{a}^{\bar{1}}_{h+1,k}}({\upsilon}-{\upsilon}^{-1})\left[{a_{h,k}+1}\atop 2\right][{{A}^{\bar{0}}+2E_{h,k}-E_{h+1,k}|{A}^{\bar{1}}-E_{h,k}}]\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle:{}_{\text{\sc sdp}}{\rm{H}}\overline{\textsc{e}}(h,{A^{\!\star}})

In general, we have
